Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The picture for the sample test. [Image]

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where there's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
3. We need to find the maximum clique size (maximum set of vertices where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

A clique in this graph means a set of points where every pair satisfies the edge condition: |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Let me think about when two points DON'T have an edge: when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For a clique, we need that for every pair (i,j) in the clique, |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Rearranging: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i

This means that in a clique, if we sort the points by x-coordinate, for any two points in the clique, the points with larger x-coordinates have larger (x + w) values.

But we need every pair to have this property. 

Actually, the condition for any two points in a clique is that for any i < j, v_j ≥ v_i.

However, we also need to ensure that the edge condition holds.

Wait, I think I made a mistake. Let me re-express:

For points i and j (i < j): edge exists if x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
This is equivalent to: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i

So if we define for each point i: v_i = x_i + w_i, then the condition becomes v_j ≥ v_i.

For three points i < j < k to form a clique, we need:
- v_j ≥ v_i

So the sequence v_i, v_j, v_k must be non-decreasing.

Therefore, if we sort points by x-coordinate, a subset forms a clique if and only if their v_i = x_i + w_i values are non-decreasing.

But we need maximum clique, so we want maximum number of points such that their (x_i + w_i) values are non-decreasing.

Actually, non-decreasing means v_j ≥ v_i for i < j.

But wait - for three points i < j < k, we need all pairs to satisfy the edge condition. The condition for every pair is that v_j ≥ v_i and v_k ≥ v_j, which implies v_k ≥ v_i.

So yes, the values v_i = x_i + w_i must be non-decreasing across the sequence.

But is that sufficient? Let me verify.

If v_j ≥ v_i for all i < j in our subset, then for any two points i and j, x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which means x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j. This is exactly the condition we need for an edge.

Therefore, the set forms a clique.

So the problem reduces to: sort points by x-coordinate, then find the longest subsequence where the values (x_i + w_i) are non-decreasing.

This is equivalent to finding the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence.

However, we can do better. Since we're dealing with values that need to be non-decreasing, and we have the sequence of points sorted by x, we can use a greedy approach or even just check the sequence directly.

But for the entire clique, we need this for every pair, not just adjacent ones.

1. Read n points
2. Sort by x-coordinate
3. For each point, compute v_i = x_i + w_i

- We can use a greedy approach with a balanced data structure
- Or we can use a simpler observation

Actually, I recall that for longest non-decreasing subsequence, we can use a greedy method that maintains the minimum end value of sequences of length k.

However, I think for this problem, there might be a simpler solution due to the specific constraints.

Wait - let me reconsider the condition again.

For two points with x-coordinates x_i and x_j (x_i < x_j), we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.
This is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then we need y_j ≥ y_i.

Therefore, in a clique, when sorted by x, the values y_i must be non-decreasing.

So we need the longest subsequence where y_i values are non-decreasing.

We can solve this using a stack-based approach or more efficiently with a Fenwick tree, but for simplicity and given constraints, I'll implement an O(n²) solution first, though it might be too slow.

Actually, for n=200,000, O(n²) is too slow. We need O(n log n).

The standard algorithm for longest non-decreasing subsequence:

```python
def longest_non_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    # We want the length of longest non-decreasing subsequence
    # Use the fact that we can maintain the minimum value at the end of sequences of given length
    # Or use patience sorting variant
    tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending value of increasing subsequence of length i+1
    
    for val in arr:
        # Binary search for the leftmost position where we can place val
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] <= val:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(val)
        else:
            tails[left] = val
    
    return len(tails)
```

This works for longest non-decreasing subsequence and is O(n log n).

But let me verify this algorithm on a small example.

Example: points = [(2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)]
Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Compute v_i = x_i + w_i:
- (0,2): v = 0+2 = 2
- (2,3): v = 2+3 = 5  
- (3,1): v = 3+1 = 4
- (6,1): v = 6+1 = 7

Sequence: [2, 5, 4, 7]

Longest non-decreasing subsequence: [2,5,7] or [2,4,7] - length 3.

This matches the expected output of 3.

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort points by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Compute x_i + w_i for each point
v_values = []
for x, w in points:
    v_values.append(x + w)

# Find length of longest non-decreasing subsequence
tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending value of non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for val in v_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where tails[pos] > val
    left, right = 0, len(tails)
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if tails[mid] <= val:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    
    if left == len(tails):
        tails.append(val)
    else:
        tails[left] = val

print(len(tails))
```

    v_values.append(x + w)

# Find length of longest non-decreasing subsequence

        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if tails[mid] <= val:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    
    if left == len(tails):
        tails.append(val)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. So for any two points i and j in our clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

However, let's think about when two points are NOT connected. They are NOT connected when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So our goal is to find the largest subset where for every pair of points, they are "far enough apart" (distance ≥ sum of weights).

x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i) ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + w_k + 2w_j > w_i + w_k

So yes, if all consecutive pairs satisfy the condition, then all pairs do.

Therefore, we need to find the longest sequence of points where for each consecutive pair (i,j), we have x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is now a simpler problem: find the longest chain in a sequence where each consecutive pair satisfies the distance condition.

We can solve this with dynamic programming:

1. Sort by x-coordinate
2. For each point i, dp[i] = 1 + max{dp[j] for j < i such that x_i - x_j ≥ w_j + w_i}
3. Answer is max(dp[i])

But this is O(n^2) which might be too slow for n=200,000.

We need a more efficient solution.

We want: for point i, we want max_{j < i and x_i - x_j ≥ w_j + w_i} dp[j]

This is equivalent to: x_i - w_i ≥ x_j + w_j

So if we define y_j = x_j + w_j, then we need x_i - w_i ≥ y_j.

And we want to maximize dp[j] for all j < i such that y_j ≤ x_i - w_i.

This is a range maximum query problem.

We can do this with a segment tree or a Fenwick tree, but we need to be careful about the values.

Actually, we can do:

1. Sort by x-coordinate
2. For each point i, we need to find max dp[j] for j < i where x_j + w_j ≤ x_i - w_i
3. We can maintain a data structure that supports range maximum queries

But the values x_j + w_j and x_i - w_i can be very large (up to 2e9).

We can use coordinate compression on the values of (x_j + w_j) and (x_i - w_i).

Algorithm:
- Sort points by x-coordinate
- Preprocess all values of (x_j + w_j) for j in 0 to n-1
- Process points in order of increasing x
- For each point i, query the maximum dp[j] for all j < i with (x_j + w_j) ≤ (x_i - w_i)
- Update the data structure with dp[i] = 1 + max_query_value

We can use a segment tree or a Fenwick tree for range maximum queries.

However, since the constraints are large, and we only need range maximum queries with a simple condition, we can use a Fenwick tree for maximum queries.

But Fenwick tree for maximum queries is not as straightforward as for sums.

Alternatively, we can use a segment tree.

But let's first verify with the example.

Example: points (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

After sorting by x: 
Point 0: x=0, w=2, y=0+2=2
Point 1: x=2, w=3, y=2+3=5
Point 2: x=3, w=1, y=3+1=4
Point 3: x=6, w=1, y=6+1=7

For each point i:
- i=0: no j < i, dp[0] = 1
- i=1: need j < 1 with y_j ≤ x_1 - w_1 = 2-3 = -1. But y_j ≥ 0, so no j. dp[1] = 1
- i=2: need j < 2 with y_j ≤ x_2 - w_2 = 3-1 = 2. y_j values: 2 (j=0), 5 (j=1). Only j=0 has y_j=2 ≤ 2. So max dp[j] = dp[0]=1. So dp[2] = 1 + 1 = 2
- i=3: need j < 3 with y_j ≤ x_3 - w_3 = 6-1 = 5. y_j values: 2, 5, 4. All ≤ 5. 
  - j=0: y=2 ≤ 5, dp=1
  - j=1: y=5 ≤ 5, dp=1  
  - j=2: y=4 ≤ 5, dp=2
  - max = 2, so dp[3] = 1 + 2 = 3

Maximum dp is 3. Correct!

So the algorithm works.

Now I'll implement it with coordinate compression and a segment tree or Fenwick tree for range maximum queries.

Since we only need range maximum queries and we have to query over the values of y_j = x_j + w_j, I'll use coordinate compression and a segment tree.

However, for simplicity and given the constraints, I'll use a Fenwick tree for maximum queries, though it has limitations.

Actually, I'll use a simple approach with sorting and processing in order.

We'll use a tree or array, but we need to handle the high values.

I'll use coordinate compression.

Steps:
1. Read input and create points
2. Sort by x
3. Extract all values of (x_j + w_j) and (x_i - w_i) for query
4. Compress coordinates
5. Use a segment tree for range maximum queries
6. Process each point in order, query the maximum dp for values ≤ (x_i - w_i), then update

But we only need to store values of (x_j + w_j) for points.

I'll implement a segment tree for range maximum query.

```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_left

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.n = size
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * size)
    
    def update(self, idx, val, l=0, r=None, node=0):
        if r is None:
            r = self.n - 1
        if l == r:
            self.tree[node] = val
            return
        mid = (l + r) // 2
        if idx <= mid:
            self.update(idx, val, l, mid, 2 * node + 1)
        else:
            self.update(idx, val, mid + 1, r, 2 * node + 2)
        self.tree[node] = max(self.tree[2 * node + 1], self.tree[2 * node + 2])
    
    def query(self, left, right, l=0, r=None, node=0):
        if r is None:
            r = self.n - 1
        if right < l or r < left:
            return 0
        if left <= l and r <= right:
            return self.tree[node]
        mid = (l + r) // 2
        left_val = 0
        right_val = 0
        if left <= mid:
            left_val = self.query(left, right, l, mid, 2 * node + 1)
        if right > mid:
            right_val = self.query(left, right, mid + 1, r, 2 * node + 2)
        return max(left_val, right_val)

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Extract all y values (x + w) and also x - w values for queries
    # We only need the values of x_j + w_j for storing in segment tree
    y_values = []
    for x, w in points:
        y_values.append(x + w)
    
    # Coordinate compression for y_values and also for x - w values
    # We need to query for values <= x_i - w_i
    # So we need all values of (x_i - w_i) as well
    query_values = []
    for x, w in points:
        query_values.append(x - w)
    
    # Combine all values for compression
    all_vals = y_values + query_values
    all_vals = list(set(all_vals))
    all_vals.sort()
    
    # Create mapping from value to index
    val_to_idx = {}
    for i, val in enumerate(all_vals):
        val_to_idx[val] = i
    
    # Segment tree size
    seg_size = len(all_vals)
    
    # DP array
    dp = [0] * n
    seg_tree = SegmentTree(seg_size)
    
    max_clique = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        
        # Query: find max dp[j] for j < i where y_j <= x - w
        query_val = x - w
        if query_val < all_vals[0]:
            max_dp = 0
        else:
            # Find the index of the largest value <= query_val
            pos = bisect_left(all_vals, query_val)
            if pos == 0:
                max_dp = 0
            else:
                # Query for values from 0 to pos-1
                max_dp = seg_tree.query(0, pos - 1)
        
        # dp[i] = 1 + max_dp
        dp[i] = 1 + max_dp
        
        # Update segment tree at index corresponding to y_i = x + w
        y_val = x + w
        idx = val_to_idx[y_val]
        seg_tree.update(idx, dp[i])
        
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)

solve()
```

-2->0, -1->1, 2->2, 4->3, 5->4, 7->5

dp[0] = 1

seg_tree: [1,0,0,0,0,0] at indices 0..5

query range [0,0]: val= -2, which has index 0, value=1

values at indices 0,1: 1,0 -> max = 1

query_val = 6-1 = 5
pos = bisect_left(all_vals, 5) = 4
query range [0,3]: values -2,-1,2,4
values at indices 0,1,2,3: 1,0,0,2 -> max = 2
dp[3] = 1+2 = 3

max_clique = 3. Correct!

One more edge case: if there's only one point.

I'll write the final code.

```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_left

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.n = size
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * size)
    
    def update(self, idx, val, l=0, r=None, node=0):
        if r is None:
            r = self.n - 1
        if l == r:
            self.tree[node] = val
            return
        mid = (l + r) // 2
        if idx <= mid:
            self.update(idx, val, l, mid, 2 * node + 1)
        else:
            self.update(idx, val, mid + 1, r, 2 * node + 2)
        self.tree[node] = max(self.tree[2 * node + 1], self.tree[2 * node + 2])
    
    def query(self, left, right, l=0, r=None, node=0):
        if r is None:
            r = self.n - 1
        if right < l or r < left:
            return 0
        if left <= l and r <= right:
            return self.tree[node]
        mid = (l + r) // 2
        left_val = 0
        right_val = 0
        if left <= mid:
            left_val = self.query(left, right, l, mid, 2 * node + 1)
        if right > mid:
            right_val = self.query(left, right, mid + 1, r, 2 * node + 2)
        return max(left_val, right_val)

def main():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    points.sort()
    
    # Extract values for coordinate compression
    y_values = []  # x_i + w_i
    query_values = []  # x_i - w_i
    
    for x, w in points:
        y_values.append(x + w)
        query_values.append(x - w)
    
    all_vals = list(set(y_values + query_values))
    all_vals.sort()
    
    val_to_idx = {val: i for i, val in enumerate(all_vals)}
    
    # Segment tree for maximum queries
    seg_size = len(all_vals)
    seg_tree = SegmentTree(seg_size)
    
    max_clique = 0
    dp = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        query_val = x - w
        
        # Find the largest index such that all_vals[index] <= query_val
        pos = bisect_left(all_vals, query_val)
        
        if pos == 0:
            max_prev_dp = 0
        else:
            max_prev_dp = seg_tree.query(0, pos - 1)
        
        dp[i] = 1 + max_prev_dp
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
        
        # Update the segment tree with the value at index corresponding to (x + w)
        y_val = x + w
        idx = val_to_idx[y_val]
        seg_tree.update(idx, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where there's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
3. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest set of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge)

## Key Insight

For a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected by an edge. That means for any two points i and j in the clique:
|x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

This condition means that points that are "far enough apart" (distance ≥ sum of weights) are connected.

However, if we think about this differently: two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So in the complement graph, two points are adjacent if they're too close (distance < sum of weights).

But we want the maximum clique in the original graph, which is equivalent to the maximum independent set in the complement graph.

## Key Observation

Let me think about when two points are NOT connected. Points i and j are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

This happens when the distance between them is less than the sum of their weights.

For points on a line, we can sort them by position. After sorting by x-coordinate, for consecutive points, we can check when they're not connected.

## Critical Insight

Actually, let me reconsider. For points on a line, if we sort them by coordinate, then for any two points i and j (i < j), we have |x_i - x_j| = x_j - x_i.

For them to be connected: x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

Rearranging: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means point j is "connected" to point i if x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define for each point i: y_i = x_i - w_i, and z_i = x_i + w_i, then the condition for connection becomes z_j ≥ z_i when x_j > x_i.

Wait, let me recast this more clearly.

Two points i and j are connected if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Without loss of generality, assume x_i ≤ x_j. Then x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, which gives x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So for points sorted by x-coordinate, point j is connected to point i if x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means that we can define a value v_i = x_i + w_i. Then point i is connected to point j (with j > i) if x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which means v_j ≥ v_i.

But wait - this is only for j > i. Let me recheck:

If i < j, then we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, so x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then point j is connected to point i if y_j ≥ y_i.

Therefore, if we sort the points by their x-coordinate, then for any two points i and j with i < j, they are connected if y_j ≥ y_i.

This means that in the sorted order, if we have points with values y_i = x_i + w_i, then points with larger y values are connected to points with smaller y values.

But for a clique, every pair must be connected. So we need a set of points where for any two points i and j in the set, we have y_j ≥ y_i or y_i ≥ y_j (depending on ordering).

Actually, if we have a set of points with y-values y_1, y_2, ..., y_k in increasing order, then for any two points, we need the condition to hold. For consecutive points, it's the critical one.

Since we need every pair to satisfy the connection condition, and the condition depends on the relative ordering, let me think differently.

## Final Insight

Actually, let's go back to sorting by x-coordinate.

After sorting by x-coordinate, we have points p_1, p_2, ..., p_n with coordinates x_1 ≤ x_2 ≤ ... ≤ x_n.

For any two points i < j, they are connected if x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

Let me define for each point i: r_i = x_i + w_i.

Then we need x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which is equivalent to r_j ≥ r_i.

So points i and j are connected if r_j ≥ r_i when j > i.

For a clique, we need every pair to be connected. 

Consider three points i < j < k. For them to be in a clique, we need:
1. Point i and j connected: r_j ≥ r_i
2. Point i and k connected: r_k ≥ r_i  
3. Point j and k connected: r_k ≥ r_j

So in fact, if we have a set of points, they form a clique if and only if their r-values are in non-decreasing order and the condition holds for all pairs.

But since r_j ≥ r_i for j > i, if we have points with r-values in increasing order, then for any i < j, we have r_j ≥ r_i, so the condition holds.

Therefore, any subset of points that have r_i = x_i + w_i in non-decreasing order forms a clique.

The maximum clique is the largest subset of these points such that their r-values are non-decreasing.

However, we also need to ensure the actual distance condition. Let me verify:

For i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we sort by x-coordinate, and then select a subset where r_j ≥ r_i for j > i (which means the r-values are non-decreasing), then the connection condition holds.

So the problem reduces to: find the longest non-decreasing subsequence of the values r_i = x_i + w_i, where the sequence is formed by sorting the points by their x-coordinates.

Wait, actually, since we're sorting by x-coordinate, and r_i = x_i + w_i, we want the longest subsequence where r_j ≥ r_i for all j > i in the subsequence.

But this is simply the longest non-decreasing subsequence (LNDS) of the r-values.

However, I need to be careful. The condition is only for pairs that are connected, and we need all pairs to have the connection.

But in fact, if all r_j ≥ r_i for j > i in our subset, then for any two points i < j in the subset, we have the connection condition satisfied.

Therefore, the maximum clique size is the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence of the values x_i + w_i when the points are sorted by x_i.

But is that correct? Let me think about it again.

Actually, I think I made a mistake. The condition for connection between i and j (i < j) is x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define r_i = x_i + w_i, then we need r_j ≥ r_i.

For a set of points to form a clique, we need that for any two points i, j in the set, r_j ≥ r_i or r_i ≥ r_j (depending on ordering).

Since we sort by x, for i < j, we need r_j ≥ r_i.

Therefore, in the sorted order, a subset forms a clique if the r-values are non-decreasing.

So the maximum clique size is the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence of r_i values.

However, this is not quite right either. The longest non-decreasing subsequence might not be the answer.

Let me reconsider with the example:

Input: 4 points
- (2,3): r = 2+3 = 5
- (3,1): r = 3+1 = 4  
- (6,1): r = 6+1 = 7
- (0,2): r = 0+2 = 2

Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1) with r values: 2, 5, 4, 7

Now we need to find the longest non-decreasing subsequence of [2,5,4,7].

Possible subsequences:
- [2] length 1
- [2,5] length 2  
- [2,4] length 2
- [2,7] length 2
- [5,7] length 2
- [2,4,7] length 3 (positions 0,2,3)

So longest non-decreasing subsequence has length 3.

Output is 3. This matches!

So the algorithm is:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, compute r_i = x_i + w_i

## Implementation

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()  # sort by x coordinate

# Extract r_i = x_i + w_i
r_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find length of longest non-decreasing subsequence
# Use patience sorting style: maintain tails where tails[i] is the minimum ending value of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1
tails = []

for r in r_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where tails[pos] > r
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(tails, r)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(r)
    else:
        tails[pos] = r

print(len(tails))
```

This should work.

Let me verify the logic:
- We want non-decreasing subsequence
- tails[i] stores the minimum possible ending value for a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1
- For each new value r, we find the first position where the value at tails is > r
- If such position doesn't exist (pos == len(tails)), we append r, extending the sequence
- Otherwise, we replace tails[pos] with r, which maintains the minimum property

This algorithm indeed computes the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence in O(n log n) time.

The answer is len(tails).


View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i. We form a graph where there's an edge between points i and j if and only if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

We need to find the size of the maximum clique in this graph.

## Key Insight

A clique is a set of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge. For our graph, two points i and j are connected if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

This means two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So, in a clique, every pair of points must satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Let me verify with the example again:

Sorted points by x:
(0,2): a=2, b=-2

(3,6): x=6-3=3, w_i+w_j=1+1=2 → 3>=2 ✓

All pairs satisfy the condition.

In our greedy algorithm, when we process point j=2 (x=3), we check if b_j >= max_a (2), which is true (2>=2), so we include it. The max_a is updated to max(2,4)=4.

When we process point j=3 (x=6), we check if b_j=5 >= max_a=4, which is true.

The condition we ensure is that for any pair i<j in the clique, b_j >= a_i (which corresponds to x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j).

Since we're processing in increasing x, and for each j, we ensure that b_j >= max_{i in clique, i<j} a_i, which is exactly the condition for all previous points i.

Therefore, the algorithm works.

Final algorithm:
1. Read n points (x, w)
2. For each point, compute a = x + w, b = x - w
3. Sort points by x-coordinate
4. Initialize max_a = 0, count = 0
5. For each point in sorted order:
   - If b >= max_a:
     - count += 1
     - max_a = max(max_a, a)
6. Output count

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Calculate a = x + w, b = x - w for each point
points_with_values = []
for x, w in points:
    a = x + w
    b = x - w
    points_with_values.append((x, a, b))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points_with_values.sort()

max_a = 0
count = 0

for x, a, b in points_with_values:
    if b >= max_a:
        count += 1
        max_a = max(max_a, a)

print(count)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We build a graph where vertices are these points
3. An edge exists between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (maximum subset of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. For points to form a clique, for every pair (i,j):
- |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

Let me think about when two points are NOT connected. Two points (i,j) are NOT connected when:
- |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j

This means we need to find a subset of points where for every pair, the distance is at least the sum of their weights.

## Strategy

The key insight is that if we sort the points by their coordinates, then for any two points, the distance is just the difference in coordinates. So if we have points sorted by x-coordinate, then for points i and j where i < j, we have |x_i - x_j| = x_j - x_i.

For points to form a clique, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j for all pairs in the subset.

Let me reframe this: if I have a subset of points, then for any two points in this subset, the distance must be at least the sum of their weights.

## Observation

For three points A, B, C in order (A at position x_a, B at x_b, C at x_c), we need:
- x_b - x_a ≥ w_a + w_b
- x_c - x_b ≥ w_b + w_c  
- x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c

Note that x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c is automatically satisfied if the first two inequalities hold, since:
x_c - x_a = (x_c - x_b) + (x_b - x_a) ≥ (w_b + w_c) + (w_a + w_b) = w_a + 2w_b + w_c > w_a + w_c

So the condition is actually weaker than what I thought. The critical condition is that for consecutive points in the sorted order, the distance must be at least the sum of their weights.

## Algorithm

1. Sort points by coordinate
2. For each point, find the maximum number of points that can form a clique ending with this point
3. We can use dynamic programming: for each point i, the maximum clique size ending at i is 1 + maximum clique size for any point j < i where |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, in our sorted order, for points i and j where i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

But if we have points in sorted order, the condition for i and j becomes x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Key Insight

For the maximum clique, if we have points in sorted order, we can try to extend the clique by including points that satisfy the distance constraint.

I think a better approach: sort by coordinate, then for each point, we want to find how many points before it satisfy that the distance is at least the sum of weights.

Actually, let me think of it differently. If we have a clique, then for any two points in it, the distance constraint must hold. 

Another key insight: if we sort by coordinate, then for any two points in the clique, the rightmost point and leftmost point must satisfy that the distance constraint. But this is hard to work with.

Let me try a different approach: since the graph structure depends on distances, and we're looking for maximum cliques, perhaps the maximum clique consists of points that are "far enough" apart.

Looking at the constraint: for points i and j, |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

If I sort by coordinate, and consider consecutive pairs, the constraint between non-consecutive pairs is automatically satisfied if consecutive ones are satisfied (as shown earlier).

So the algorithm:
1. Sort points by coordinate
2. For each point, maintain the maximum clique size ending at that point
3. For each point i, the clique size ending at i is 1 + maximum of (clique size ending at j) for all j < i such that x_i - x_j ≥ w_i + w_j

But this is O(n^2) and n can be 200,000, so it's too slow.

## Optimization

I need to optimize the query. For a fixed point i, I want to find the maximum clique size among points j < i such that x_i - x_j ≥ w_i + w_j.

This can be rewritten as: x_j ≤ x_i - (w_i + w_j)

The constraint x_j ≤ x_i - w_i - w_j is complex because w_j is also in the constraint.

I need to find j such that x_j ≤ x_i - w_i - w_j.

Rearranging: x_j + w_j ≤ x_i - w_i

So for each point i, I need to find the maximum clique size among points j < i such that x_j + w_j ≤ x_i - w_i.

Now the problem becomes: for each point i, I want to query the maximum clique size among all points j with x_j + w_j ≤ (x_i - w_i).

I can solve this with coordinate compression and a data structure (like a segment tree or binary indexed tree) to maintain the maximum value.

However, there's a simpler pattern: maybe the maximum clique is found by considering points with small x_j + w_j values.

Actually, I think there's a greedy approach: sort points by x + w, and then use a data structure to query the maximum clique size efficiently.

Wait, let me reconsider the constraint: x_j + w_j ≤ x_i - w_i.

For a fixed i, the condition is x_j + w_j ≤ x_i - w_i.

So I want to query points j < i with (x_j + w_j) ≤ (x_i - w_i), and return the maximum of dp[j].

I can process points in order of increasing x_i, and maintain a data structure keyed by (x_j + w_j).

This is a classic setting for offline queries with a segment tree or Fenwick tree.

But let me see if there's a simpler insight.

## Final Approach

1. Sort points by coordinate x_i
2. For each point i, compute the value x_i - w_i (this is key for the constraint)
3. We want to find maximum dp[j] for all j < i such that x_j + w_j ≤ (x_i - w_i)
4. Use a data structure (like a Fenwick tree or segment tree) to maintain the maximum value for each (x_j + w_j) value

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # dp[i] will store the size of maximum clique ending at point i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    # We'll use coordinate compression for x + w values
    x_plus_w_values = []
    for x, w in points:
        x_plus_w_values.append(x + w)
    
    # Sort the values for coordinate compression
    sorted_values = sorted(set(x_plus_w_values))
    value_to_idx = {val: idx for idx, val in enumerate(sorted_values)}
    
    # Use a Fenwick tree (Binary Indexed Tree) for maximum queries
    # Actually, we need a segment tree for range maximum queries
    
    # Since we only need point updates and range maximum queries, we can use a segment tree
    # But for simplicity, we'll implement a list and update it as we go, or use a different approach
    
    # Alternative insight: since the constraint is x_j + w_j <= x_i - w_i,
    # and we want max dp[j] for j < i, we can just iterate and keep track of the maximum dp value
    # for each possible (x_j + w_j) value as we go.
    
    # But we still need to query based on (x_j + w_j) <= (x_i - w_i)
    
    # Let's use a list to store the maximum dp value for each x_plus_w value
    # We'll maintain an array where index corresponds to compressed value of x + w
    
    # Initialize the tree
    size = len(sorted_values)
    tree = [0] * (size + 1)  # 1-indexed
    
    def update(idx, val):
        # Update the tree at position idx with value val (take maximum)
        while idx <= size:
            tree[idx] = max(tree[idx], val)
            idx += idx & (-idx)
    
    def query(idx):
        # Query maximum value in [1, idx]
        result = 0
        while idx > 0:
            result = max(result, tree[idx])
            idx -= idx & (-idx)
        return result
    
    # Process points in order of increasing x
    max_clique = 1
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        target = x - w  # we need x_j + w_j <= target
        
        # Find the maximum index in sorted_values such that value <= target
        # Binary search for the largest value <= target
        left, right = 0, len(sorted_values) - 1
        compressed_target = -1
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if sorted_values[mid] <= target:
                compressed_target = mid
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        if compressed_target == -1:
            # No point satisfies the constraint
            dp[i] = 1
        else:
            # Query the maximum dp[j] for points j < i with x_j + w_j <= sorted_values[compressed_target]
            prev_max = query(compressed_target + 1)  # +1 for 1-indexed
            dp[i] = prev_max + 1
        
        # Update the tree with current point
        x_plus_w = x + w
        pos = value_to_idx[x_plus_w]
        update(pos + 1, dp[i])
        
        # Update global maximum
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)

solve()
```

```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by coordinate
    points.sort()

    dp = [1] * n
    
    # Collect all x + w values for coordinate compression
    x_plus_w_values = []
    for x, w in points:
        x_plus_w_values.append(x + w)
    
    # Sort unique values
    unique_vals = sorted(set(x_plus_w_values))
    val_to_idx = {val: i for i, val in enumerate(unique_vals)}
    
    # Fenwick tree for maximum queries (segment tree for range maximum queries)
    size = len(unique_vals)
    tree = [0] * (size + 1)
    
    def update(idx, val):
        # Update index idx (1-indexed) with value val
        while idx <= size:
            tree[idx] = max(tree[idx], val)
            idx += idx & (-idx)
    
    def query(idx):
        # Query maximum value in range [1, idx] (1-indexed)
        result = 0
        while idx > 0:
            result = max(result, tree[idx])
            idx -= idx & (-idx)
        return result
    
    max_clique = 1
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        target = x - w  # we need x_j + w_j <= target
        
        # Find the largest value in unique_vals that is <= target
        # Binary search
        left, right = 0, len(unique_vals) - 1
        best_idx = -1
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if unique_vals[mid] <= target:
                best_idx = mid
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        if best_idx == -1:
            # No point satisfies the constraint
            dp[i] = 1
        else:
            # Query the maximum dp[j] for points j < i with x_j + w_j <= unique_vals[best_idx]
            prev_max = query(best_idx + 1)  # +1 for 1-indexed
            dp[i] = prev_max + 1

        x_plus_w_idx = val_to_idx[x_plus_w]
        update(x_plus_w_idx + 1, dp[i])
        
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)
```

import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # dp[i] will store the size of maximum clique ending at point i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    # Collect all x + w values for coordinate compression
    x_plus_w_values = []
    for x, w in points:
        x_plus_w_values.append(x + w)
    
    # Sort unique values
    unique_vals = sorted(set(x_plus_w_values))
    val_to_idx = {val: i for i, val in enumerate(unique_vals)}
    
    # Fenwick tree for maximum queries (segment tree for range maximum queries)
    size = len(unique_vals)
    tree = [0] * (size + 1)
    
    def update(idx, val):
        # Update index idx (1-indexed) with value val
        while idx <= size:
            tree[idx] = max(tree[idx], val)
            idx += idx & (-idx)
    
    def query(idx):
        # Query maximum value in range [1, idx] (1-indexed)
        result = 0
        while idx > 0:
            result = max(result, tree[idx])
            idx -= idx & (-idx)
        return result
    
    max_clique = 1
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        target = x - w  # we need x_j + w_j <= target
        
        # Find the largest value in unique_vals that is <= target
        # Binary search
        left, right = 0, len(unique_vals) - 1
        best_idx = -1
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if unique_vals[mid] <= target:
                best_idx = mid
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        if best_idx == -1:
            # No point satisfies the constraint
            dp[i] = 1
        else:
            # Query the maximum dp[j] for points j < i with x_j + w_j <= unique_vals[best_idx]
            prev_max = query(best_idx + 1)  # +1 for 1-indexed
            dp[i] = prev_max + 1
        
        # Update the tree with current point
        x_plus_w = x + w
        x_plus_w_idx = val_to_idx[x_plus_w]
        update(x_plus_w_idx + 1, dp[i])
        
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest set of vertices where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. So for any two vertices i and j in the clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

However, if we have three points A, B, C with coordinates x_A, x_B, x_C (sorted), then the condition must hold for all pairs:
- |x_A - x_B| ≥ w_A + w_B
- |x_A - x_C| ≥ w_A + w_C  
- |x_B - x_C| ≥ w_B + w_C

Since the points are on a line, we can sort them by coordinate. Let's sort the points by x-coordinate.

## Key Observation

For three points in sorted order (A, B, C) where x_A < x_B < x_C:
- |x_A - x_C| = x_C - x_A
- The condition for A and C is x_C - x_A ≥ w_A + w_C
- The condition for B and C is x_C - x_B ≥ w_B + w_C
- The condition for A and B is x_B - x_A ≥ w_A + w_B

But since x_C - x_A ≥ x_C - x_B and x_C - x_A ≥ x_B - x_A, if we can find a local constraint that governs all pairs.

## Critical Insight

For any two points i and j, we have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

When we have a set of points in order, the most restrictive constraint will be between the farthest points.

However, let's think about when two points are NOT connected: when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So two points are NOT connected if they are "too close" in terms of distance vs sum of weights.

Therefore, a clique is a set of points where every pair satisfies the distance condition.

## Key Optimization

I can use a greedy approach or dynamic programming. But there's a much simpler observation:

If points are sorted by x-coordinate, then for any three points (i,j,k) where i < j < k, the condition for i and k is x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k.

For the set to be a clique, we need all pairs to satisfy the condition.

But here's the key insight: if we sort the points by x-coordinate, and consider the "distance constraint", then a necessary and sufficient condition for three points to be in a clique is that the leftmost and rightmost points satisfy the distance constraint.

Wait, let me reconsider.

Actually, the condition is: |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

When points are sorted by x-coordinate, for points i < j < k:
- x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j (must hold)
- x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k (must hold) 
- x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k (must hold)

Since x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i), we have:
x_k - x_i ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k

But we only need the direct constraints. 

## Better Approach

I can use the fact that the graph complement has edges when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

But that might be complex.

Actually, let me think differently. For a clique to exist, any two points must satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

With sorted coordinates, for any three points, the constraint between the leftmost and rightmost is the most demanding.

But I think there's a simpler way: I can use a sliding window approach.

## Final Insight

After thinking about this problem, I recall that for this type of problem with points on a line, a key insight is:

For a set of points to form a clique, the condition must hold for every pair. 

But due to the structure, if I sort the points by x-coordinate, then for any three consecutive points, I can check if they can be in a clique.

Actually, here's the correct approach:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each possible clique size, check if it's possible
3. Use the fact that if points i < j < k in sorted order, then |x_i - x_k| ≥ w_i + w_k must hold, and also |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j and |x_j - x_k| ≥ w_j + w_k.

However, since x_k - x_i ≥ x_k - x_j and x_k - x_i ≥ x_j - x_i, if |x_k - x_j| ≥ w_j + w_k and |x_j - x_i| ≥ w_i + w_j, it might not imply |x_k - x_i| ≥ w_i + w_k.

But in fact, |x_k - x_i| = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i) ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k, which is ≥ w_i + w_k.

So if the two inner constraints hold, the outer constraint automatically holds.

Therefore, for a set of points in sorted order to form a clique, it's sufficient to check that every consecutive pair satisfies |x_j - x_i| ≥ w_i + w_j.

This means I can iterate through all possible subsets and check this condition.

But with n up to 200,000, I need an efficient solution.

## Efficient Approach

I can use dynamic programming. Let me sort by x-coordinate.

For each point i, I can consider the longest sequence ending at i where every consecutive pair satisfies the distance constraint.

But I need to check all pairs, not just consecutive.

Actually, the key insight is that if I have a set of points forming a clique, and I sort them by coordinate, then the condition must hold for consecutive pairs.

This is because if consecutive pairs hold, then any non-consecutive pair will automatically hold (as shown above).

Therefore, I can:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, find the longest sequence ending at that point where for every consecutive pair (i, j) in the sequence, |x_j - x_i| ≥ w_i + w_j

This can be done with a greedy approach or dynamic programming.

Let me implement:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

max_clique = 1

# For each possible starting point, extend the clique
for i in range(n):
    # dp[j] = length of longest clique ending at j, starting from some point
    # But we want to extend from i
    current_end = i
    for j in range(i + 1, n):
        # Check if points i and j can be in the same clique
        # They need to satisfy |x_j - x_i| >= w_i + w_j
        if points[j][0] - points[i][0] >= points[i][1] + points[j][1]:
            current_end = j
            # All points from i to j can form a clique if all consecutive pairs satisfy the condition
            # But we need to check the actual sequence
            # Actually, since we're extending step by step, we just check consecutive
            # But here we're not checking non-consecutive, but if consecutive pairs work, all work
            # So we just need to check each step
            # In this nested loop, we're checking each consecutive pair
            # But we need to ensure the entire sequence is valid
            # Actually, if we have points i, j, k with i<j<k, we need:
            # x_j-x_i >= w_i+w_j and x_k-x_j >= w_j+w_k and x_k-x_i >= w_i+w_k
            # And we know x_k-x_i >= (x_k-x_j) + (x_j-x_i) >= (w_j+w_k) + (w_i+w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k >= w_i + w_k
            # So the outer constraint is automatically satisfied if inner ones are
            # Therefore, it's sufficient to check consecutive pairs
            pass
    # After finishing the inner loop, the length of clique from i to current_end is current_end - i + 1
    if current_end >= i:
        max_clique = max(max_clique, current_end - i + 1)

print(max_clique)
```

Wait, this approach has an issue. The nested loop above doesn't correctly check the consecutive pairs. Let me fix it.

Let me go back to the mathematical proof.

If I have three points in sorted order: i < j < k
- Condition 1: x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
- Condition 2: x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k
- Then: x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i) ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k ≥ w_i + w_k

So the condition for (i,k) is automatically satisfied.

Therefore, if all consecutive pairs in a sorted sequence satisfy the distance condition, then all pairs in the sequence satisfy the condition.

Therefore, a set of points can form a clique if and only if in the sorted order, every consecutive pair satisfies the distance constraint.

Therefore, I can:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each possible starting point, find the longest sequence of consecutive points (in sorted order) where each consecutive pair satisfies the distance constraint
3. The size of the longest such sequence is the answer

So we can do:

```python
n = int(input())

    end = start

        # This requires that (end, next_idx) satisfy the condition
        if points[next_idx][0] - points[end][0] >= points[end][1] + points[next_idx][1]:
            end = next_idx
        # Otherwise, we cannot add next_idx to the sequence
        # But we don't need to do anything; we continue to next_idx
    max_clique = max(max_clique, end - start + 1)
```

I think I have a fundamental design flaw.

I think I need to reset my approach.

After research and thinking, I recall that for this type of problem, the maximum clique can be found by considering the sorted order and then using a greedy or dynamic programming approach to find the longest subsequence where each consecutive pair in the subsequence satisfies the distance condition.

The sequence of points in the sorted order (by coordinate) forms a path, and we want the longest path where each edge (consecutive in the path) satisfies the distance condition.

This means we need to find the longest subsequence of the sorted points (by coordinate) such that for every consecutive pair in the subsequence, the distance between them is at least the sum of their weights.

This is a longest subsequence problem, which can be solved with dynamic programming in O(n^2), but n can be 200,000.

We need a linear or O(n log n) solution.

Perhaps we can use a greedy approach: when we have a chain, we can extend it by adding a point if it satisfies the condition with the last point.

But we want the longest such sequence.

We can do:

- Sort by coordinate
- For each possible end point, we want to know the longest chain ending at that point.

Let dp[i] = the length of the longest chain ending at point i.

Then dp[i] = 1 + max{ dp[j] | j < i and |x_i - x_j| >= w_j + w_i }

We can optimize by noting that for a fixed i, we only need to consider j such that x_i - x_j >= w_j + w_i.

This can be rewritten as: x_i - w_i >= x_j + w_j.

So for each point i, we want to find the maximum dp[j] for j < i such that x_j + w_j <= x_i - w_i.

This is a range query: we want to update a data structure with (x_j + w_j, dp[j]) and query for the maximum dp[j] for values of (x_j + w_j) <= (x_i - w_i).

We can use a Fenwick tree or segment tree with coordinate compression.

The algorithm:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point i (0 to n-1):
   - Let threshold = x_i - w_i
   - Query the maximum dp[j] for all j < i with x_j + w_j <= threshold
   - dp[i] = 1 + query result
3. Answer = max(dp[i]) for all i

Let me test with the example:

Points: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Compute for each i:

i=0: (0,2)
- threshold = 0 - 2 = -2
- no j < 0, so dp[0] = 1

i=1: (2,3)
- threshold = 2 - 3 = -1
- j=0: x0+w0 = 0+2 = 2 > -1? 2 > -1 → not <= -1 → no
- dp[1] = 1

i=2: (3,1)
- threshold = 3 - 1 = 2
- j=0: x0+w0=0+2=2 <= 2 → yes, dp[0]=1
- j=1: x1+w1=2+3=5 > 2 → no
- dp[2] = 1 + 1 = 2

i=3: (6,1)
- threshold = 6 - 1 = 5
- j=0: 0+2=2 <= 5 → dp[0]=1
- j=1: 2+3=5 <= 5 → dp[1]=1
- j=2: 3+1=4 <= 5 → dp[2]=2
- dp[3] = 1 + max(1,1,2) = 3

Max dp[i] = 3

This matches the expected output of 3.

The clique is points at indices 0,2,3: (0,2), (3,1), (6,1)

Check pairs:
- (0,3): |3-0|=3 >= 2+1=3 → yes
- (0,6): |6-0|=6 >= 2+1=3 → yes
- (3,6): |6-3|=3 >= 1+1=2 → yes

Perfect.

Implementation with Fenwick tree or segment tree for maximum query.

Since we only need maximum, we can use a Fenwick tree for maximum queries.

Steps:
1. Coordinate compress the values of (x_j + w_j) for all j
2. Use a segment tree or Fenwick tree for range maximum query
3. For each i, query the max dp[j] for x_j + w_j <= x_i - w_i
4. Update the tree at position (x_i + w_i) with value dp[i]

Note: the values x_i - w_i can be negative, so we need to handle negative coordinates.

We can shift coordinates by a constant or use a coordinate compression that includes all values.

Let's do coordinate compression on the values of (x_j + w_j) and (x_i - w_i) for all points.

We'll collect all values and compress them.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Collect all values for coordinate compression
values = set()
for x, w in points:
    values.add(x + w)
    values.add(x - w)

# Also include negative infinity and positive infinity, but we can work with the set
# Convert to sorted list
vals = sorted(values)

# Build a mapping from value to index
val_to_idx = {val: i for i, val in enumerate(vals)}

# Fenwick tree for maximum queries (1-indexed)
size = len(vals)
tree = [0] * (size + 1)

def update(idx, val):
    while idx <= size:
        tree[idx] = max(tree[idx], val)
        idx += idx & (-idx)

def query(idx):
    # Query maximum in [0, idx-1] (1-indexed)
    res = 0
    while idx > 0:
        res = max(res, tree[idx])
        idx -= idx & (-idx)
    return res

# Process each point
max_clique = 0
for i in range(n):
    x, w = points[i]
    threshold = x - w
    # Need to find all j < i such that x_j + w_j <= threshold
    # Find the largest value in vals that is <= threshold
    # Use binary search to find the index
    # Since vals is sorted, we can binary search
    # Find the rightmost position where val <= threshold
    left, right = 0, size
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if vals[mid] <= threshold:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    # left is the first index where vals[left] > threshold
    # so query up to left-1
    pos = left - 1  # index in vals for the last value <= threshold
    if pos < 0:
        prev_max = 0
    else:
        prev_max = query(pos)
    
    # dp[i] = 1 + prev_max
    dp_i = 1 + prev_max
    max_clique = max(max_clique, dp_i)
    
    # Update the tree at position corresponding to (x_i + w_i)
    val = x + w
    if val in val_to_idx:
        idx_val = val_to_idx[val]
        update(idx_val + 1, dp_i)  # 1-indexed

print(max_clique)
```

This should work. Let me test with the example.

But note: in the update, we are updating at (x_i + w_i), and the query is for values <= (x_i - w_i).

In the example:
- i=0: (0,2)
  - threshold = 0-2 = -2
  - values in vals: {0+2=2, 0-2=-2, 2+3=5, 2-3=-1, 3+1=4, 3-1=2, 6+1=7, 6-1=5} → {-2,-1,2,4,5,7}
  - threshold = -2, so values <= -2: only -2
  - pos: find first index where vals[i] > -2: at i=0: -2, i=1: -1 > -2 → so left=1, pos=0
  - query(0) = 0 (since pos=0, we do query(0) which is before 1, so returns 0)
  - dp[0] = 1
  - update at val=2: index in vals: 2 appears at index 2 (0:-2,1:-1,2:2) → so idx_val=2, update at 3
  - tree[3] = 1

- i=1: (2,3)
  - threshold = 2-3 = -1
  - values <= -1: -2, -1
  - left: first index with val > -1: at index 1: -1, index2:2> -1 → left=2, pos=1
  - query(1): values in tree at indices 1 (which corresponds to val=-1) — we have only updated at val=2 (index2), which is > -1, so not in range.
  - query(1) returns 0
  - dp[1] = 1
  - update at val=5: val_to_idx[5] = index of 5 in vals: vals: -2,-1,2,4,5,7 → index 4, so update at 5

- i=2: (3,1)
  - threshold = 3-1 = 2
  - values <= 2: -2,-1,2
  - left: first index with val>2: index of 2 is at index2, next is 4>2, so left=3, pos=2
  - query(2): values up to index2 (val=2)
  - tree: indices 1,2,3: we have updated at 3 (val=2) with value 1, and at 5 (val=5) with value 1
  - at index2 (val=2): we have value 1 from dp[0]
  - query(2) returns 1
  - dp[2] = 2
  - update at val=4: val_to_idx[4]=3, update at 4

- i=3: (6,1)
  - threshold = 6-1=5
  - values <=5: -2,-1,2,4,5
  - left: first index with val>5: index of 7, so left=5, pos=4
  - query(4): values up to index4 (val=5)
  - tree: at index1 (val=-1):0, index2 (val=2):1, index3 (val=4):1 (from dp[1]), index4 (val=5):1 (from dp[1])
  - the maximum among these is 1 (from val=2) and 1 (from val=4) and 1 (from val=5) and val=2 has 1, but we have dp[0]=1 at val=2, dp[1]=1 at val=5, dp[2]=2 at val=4
  - val=4: dp[2]=2
  - so query(4) should return 2
  - dp[3] = 1+2 = 3

Yes, it works.

Edge case: when no values, returns 0.

So the final code is:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Collect all values for coordinate compression
values = set()
for x, w in points:
    values.add(x + w)
    values.add(x - w)

# Convert to sorted list
vals = sorted(values)

# Build a mapping from value to index
val_to_idx = {val: i for i, val in enumerate(vals)}

# Fenwick tree for maximum queries (1-indexed)
size = len(vals)
tree = [0] * (size + 1)

def update(idx, val):
    while idx <= size:
        tree[idx] = max(tree[idx], val)
        idx += idx & (-idx)

def query(idx):
    # Query maximum in [0, idx-1] (1-indexed)
    res = 0
    while idx > 0:
        res = max(res, tree[idx])
        idx -= idx & (-idx)
    return res

max_clique = 0
for i in range(n):
    x, w = points[i]
    threshold = x - w
    # Find the largest index in vals such that vals[idx] <= threshold
    # Binary search for the upper bound
    left, right = 0, size
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if vals[mid] <= threshold:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    # left is the first index with vals[left] > threshold
    pos = left - 1  # position in the vals for the last value <= threshold
    if pos < 0:
        prev_max = 0
    else:
        prev_max = query(pos)
    
    dp_i = 1 + prev_max
    max_clique = max(max_clique, dp_i)
    
    # Update the tree at position for (x_i + w_i)
    val = x + w
    if val in val_to_idx:
        idx_val = val_to_idx[val]
        update(idx_val + 1, dp_i)  # 1-indexed

print(max_clique)
```

Note: the values might be repeated, and we are including them in the set, which is correct.

We use the standard Fenwick tree for maximum queries, with the update and query functions.

This should work for the given constraints. The complexity is O(n log n) due to sorting and binary search.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The picture for the sample test. [Image]

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are the points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the size of the maximum clique in this graph

## Key Insight

A clique is a set of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge. In this problem, two points i and j are NOT connected (no edge) when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So, we need to find the largest subset of points where every pair satisfies |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Key Observation

For two points to NOT be connected, we need |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to: x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j (if x_j > x_i) or x_i - x_j < w_i + w_j (if x_i > x_j)

Rearranging: x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i (if x_j > x_i) or x_i - w_i < x_j + w_j (if x_i > x_j)

Wait, let me think differently.

The condition for NO edge between i and j is: |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j

This means the points are "close enough" that they don't satisfy the clique condition.

So in a clique, every pair must satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Alternative Insight

Let me consider when two points CAN be in the same clique. Two points i and j can be in the same clique if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

But for a clique, EVERY pair must satisfy this condition.

So I need to find the largest subset where for every pair (i,j), |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Key Insight: Monotonicity and Sorting

Let me sort the points by their x-coordinates.

Suppose we have points sorted by x-coordinate: p1, p2, ..., pn.

For any two points pi and pj where i < j, we need |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j, which means x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This can be rewritten as: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So for every pair i,j with i < j, we need x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means that for a set of points to form a clique, the values (x_i - w_i) must be greater than or equal to (x_j + w_j) for all pairs where i < j.

Actually, let me correct this: for every pair i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

Which gives us: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if I define y_i = x_i + w_i, and z_i = x_i - w_i, then for a subset of points to form a clique, for any two points i < j in the subset, we need z_j ≥ y_i.

Wait, that's not quite right either.

From x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, we get x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if I define a_i = x_i + w_i and b_i = x_i - w_i, then for any two points i < j in the clique, we need b_j ≥ a_i.

For a set S to be a clique, for every pair (i,j) in S where i < j, we need b_j ≥ a_i.

This means in the sorted order, for every pair i < j in S, b_j ≥ a_i.

Actually, I can simplify this by sorting the points by x-coordinates, and then checking pairs.

But there's a simpler way: since we need every pair to satisfy the condition, this means that within the clique, the values (x_i - w_i) must be appropriately spaced.

## Better Insight

After reading about this type of problem, I recall that in graphs where edges exist when points are "far apart", the maximum clique can often be found by considering the ordering and using a greedy approach.

Actually, let me think about it differently. The condition |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j means that the distance between points must be at least the sum of their weights.

In a clique, every pair must satisfy this condition.

A key insight is that if we sort points by x-coordinate, then for any two points in the clique, the one with larger x must satisfy the distance condition with the one with smaller x.

So if we have points sorted by x-coordinate, and we want a clique, then for any two points i < j in the clique, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This can be rewritten as: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So define for each point i: d_i = x_i + w_i and e_i = x_i - w_i.

Then the condition becomes e_j ≥ d_i for all pairs i < j in the clique.

This means that in the sorted order, for any two points in the clique, the e-value of the right point must be at least the d-value of the left point.

To maximize the clique size, I can:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each position, maintain the minimum e-value seen so far
3. For each point, check if its e-value is ≥ the max d-value of previous points

Actually, let me implement a simpler approach:

Sort points by x-coordinate. Then for each possible clique, I need to ensure that for every pair, the distance condition holds.

But I can do this greedily: iterate through sorted points and maintain a set of valid candidates.

## Algorithm

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, we can try to extend a clique
3. A valid clique is a subset where for every pair, x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j when x_j > x_i

But I need to check all pairs - that would be O(n²).

Better insight: after sorting, for a set to form a clique, the condition must hold between consecutive points.

Actually, if all pairs satisfy the condition, then in particular consecutive pairs do.

But is it sufficient? No, because non-consecutive pairs might not satisfy it even if consecutive ones do.

Let me reconsider.

From the condition x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j (for j > i), we get x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if I define for each point: s_i = x_i + w_i and t_i = x_i - w_i, then for any i < j in the clique, we need t_j ≥ s_i.

This is equivalent to: for all i < j in the clique, t_j ≥ s_i.

This means that if I consider the values s_i and t_i, then in the clique, t_j must be at least as large as s_i for all previous points.

Actually, since we need t_j ≥ s_i for all i < j, this means that for the rightmost point in the clique, its t-value must be at least the maximum s-value among all previous points in the clique.

So I can use a greedy algorithm:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. Initialize an empty list for the clique candidates
3. For each point in sorted order:
   - Check if this point can be added to the current clique
   - To add point j, we need that for all previous points i in the clique, t_j ≥ s_i
   - This is equivalent to t_j ≥ max(s_i for i in clique)
4. Keep track of the maximum clique size

But actually, since we want any subset, and we sort by x-coordinate, we can try to extend the clique by adding points that satisfy the condition with all previous points.

Let me implement this:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# For each point i, define s_i = x_i + w_i and t_i = x_i - w_i
# For a clique, for any i < j, we need t_j >= s_i
max_clique = 0

# Try all possible subsets? Too expensive.

# Instead, use greedy: sort and then extend
# We'll maintain the current clique as a list of indices
# For each new point, check if it can be added to any subset

# Actually, I recall that in such problems, the maximum clique can be found by
# considering that the condition is equivalent to t_j >= max(s_i) for all i < j in clique

# Let's try a greedy approach: iterate through points and maintain the set of valid candidates

# Actually, I can do: for each point, how many previous points can it "cover"
# But I think the maximum clique is simply the size of the largest subset where 
# t_j >= max(s_i) for all i in subset with i < j

# Alternatively, I can simulate: 
# Let's maintain the maximum s-value seen so far in the current candidate clique
# When I process a point j, if t_j >= current_max_s, then I can add it to the clique
# And update current_max_s to max(current_max_s, s_j)

# But this doesn't work because we need t_j >= s_i for all i < j, not just max.

# Actually, if I maintain the maximum s-value among all points in the clique so far,
# then the condition t_j >= max_s is equivalent to t_j >= s_i for all i in clique.

# So this greedy approach works!

current_max_s = -1
clique_size = 0

for x, w in points:
    s_val = x + w
    t_val = x - w
    
    # Check if we can add this point
    if t_val >= current_max_s:
        # We can add this point to the clique
        clique_size += 1
        # Update the current maximum s-value
        current_max_s = max(current_max_s, s_val)

print(clique_size)
```

Let me reexamine the condition.

I think I made a sign error. The condition is x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j for j > i.

With x_j > x_i, this is x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j, so x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

So t_j >= s_i is correct.

But in my example, why is the answer 3?

Let me check the pairs:
Points: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Check which pairs satisfy |x_i - x_j| >= w_i + w_j

(0,2) and (2,3): |2-0|=2, w_i+w_j=2+3=5, 2<5 → no edge → cannot be in same clique
(0,2) and (3,1): |3-0|=3, 2+1=3, 3>=3 → yes
(0,2) and (6,1): |6-0|=6, 2+1=3, 6>=3 → yes

(2,3) and (3,1): |3-2|=1, 3+1=4, 1<4 → no edge
(2,3) and (6,1): |6-2|=4, 3+1=4, 4>=4 → yes
(3,1) and (6,1): |6-3|=3, 1+1=2, 3>=2 → yes

So pairs that have edges:
(0,2)-(3,1), (0,2)-(6,1), (2,3)-(6,1), (3,1)-(6,1)

Now, can I form a clique?
- (0,2), (3,1), (6,1): check pairs
  - (0,2)-(3,1): yes
  - (0,2)-(6,1): yes  
  - (3,1)-(6,1): yes
- This works! Size 3.

So the maximum clique is size 3.

My algorithm failed because it didn't consider that the order doesn't have to be consecutive in the x-sorted order.

I need a different approach.

## Correct Insight

The condition for two points to be connected is |x_i - x_j| >= w_i + w_j.

For a clique, every pair must satisfy this.

In the sorted order, for points i < j, we need x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j.

Which gives x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

So for a set S of points (sorted by x), we need that for every pair i < j in S, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

This means that the value (x_j - w_j) for the rightmost point in the set must be >= (x_i + w_i) for all left points.

So if I define for each point i: v_i = x_i + w_i (the "lower bound" for the right points)

And I have for each point j in the set: u_j = x_j - w_j (the "upper bound" for the left points)

For the set to be valid, we need that for every pair i < j in the set, u_j >= v_i.

This is equivalent to: u_j >= max(v_i) for all i < j.

So I can use a greedy algorithm:

- Sort points by x-coordinate
- For each point in sorted order, maintain the maximum v-value seen so far
- The point can be added if u_j >= current_max_v
- When added, update current_max_v = max(current_max_v, v_j)

But this is exactly what I tried earlier, and it gave 2 instead of 3.

Let me trace with the example:

Points sorted: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Define v_i = x_i + w_i, u_i = x_i - w_i

(0,2): v=2, u=-2
- current_max_v = -∞, u=-2 >= -∞ → yes
- add it, current_max_v = max(-∞, 2) = 2

(2,3): v=5, u=-1
- u=-1 >= 2? No → skip

(3,1): v=4, u=2
- u=2 >= 2? Yes → add it, current_max_v = max(2,4) = 4

(6,1): v=7, u=5
- u=5 >= 4? Yes → add it, current_max_v = max(4,7) = 7

Count = 3

Yes! This gives 3.

I made a mistake in my manual trace earlier. The condition is u_j >= current_max_v, not u_j >= current_max_v before adding.

In the first point: (0,2), v=2, u=-2
- current_max_v is initially -∞, so -2 >= -∞ is true
- We add it, and current_max_v becomes 2

For (2,3): v=5, u=-1
- u=-1 >= 2? False → skip

For (3,1): v=4, u=2
- u=2 >= 2? True → add it, current_max_v = max(2,4) = 4

For (6,1): v=7, u=5
- u=5 >= 4? True → add it, current_max_v = max(4,7) = 7

Final count = 3.

Perfect!

The algorithm is:
1. Read n points
2. Sort by x-coordinate
3. For each point, compute v = x + w and u = x - w
4. Maintain current_max_v = -1 (or negative infinity)
5. For each point in sorted order:
   - If u >= current_max_v, we can add this point to the clique
   - Increment clique size
   - Update current_max_v = max(current_max_v, v)
6. Output clique size

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

max_clique = 0
current_max_v = float('-inf')

for x, w in points:
    v = x + w
    u = x - w
    
    if u >= current_max_v:
        max_clique += 1
        current_max_v = max(current_max_v, v)

print(max_clique)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout
